```
"""System prompt sent to Qwen at the start of every conversation."""

from tgv_analyst.tools import COLUMN_DESCRIPTIONS

# The column list is written in the prompt so the model doesn't guess names.
# It comes from tools.py, so there is only one list to keep up to date.
COLUMNS_TEXT = "\n".join(f"- {name}: {meaning}" for name, meaning in COLUMN_DESCRIPTIONS.items())

# Facts about the data that the model may repeat without a tool (e.g. "2018 to 2026").
# The guard trusts the numbers of this sentence only, not the rest of the prompt:
# the rule numbers and the "13.1 %" example must stay unverified.
```

```
import sys, json, pathlib
from tgv_analyst.agent import run_agent

think = sys.argv[1] == "on"

label = sys.argv[3]

for i in range(n):
    r = run_agent(Q, think=think, trace_file=tf)
    t = r["trace"]
    tools = [c["name"] for s in t["steps"] for c in s.get("tool_calls", [])]
    errs = sum(1 for s in t["steps"] for c in s.get("tool_calls", []) if "Error" in c["result"])
```

```
"""Brick 3: reject any answer containing a number that no tool computed.

This rule is enforced by code, not by the prompt: the agent calls
check_answer() before showing an answer, whatever the model says.
"""

import re

# A comma followed by exactly 3 digits is a thousands separator: 12,792 or 1,234,567.5
THOUSANDS_PATTERN = re.compile(r"\d{1,3}(?:,\d{3})+(?:\.\d+)?(?!\d)")
# Matches 12,792 (thousands) first, otherwise 12, 12.5 and 12,5 (French decimal comma).
# The sign is ignored.
NUMBER_PATTERN = re.compile(THOUSANDS_PATTERN.pattern + r"|\d+(?:[.,]\d+)?")


def extract_numbers(text):
    """Return the numbers written in a text, as strings with a "." decimal point.

    "12,792" -> "12792" (thousands separator), "12,5" -> "12.5" (decimal comma).
    Limit: "12,500" is always read as twelve thousand five hundred, never as 12.5.
    """
    numbers = []
    for match in NUMBER_PATTERN.findall(text):
        if THOUSANDS_PATTERN.fullmatch(match):
            numbers.append(match.replace(",", ""))
        else:
            numbers.append(match.replace(",", "."))
    return numbers


def is_supported(number, known_numbers):
    """True if `number` equals a known number rounded to the same decimals.

    Example: the tool printed 0.84567, so the answer may say 0.85 or 0.846,
    but not 0.84.
    """
    decimals = len(number.split(".")[1]) if "." in number else 0
    value = float(number)
    return any(round(float(known), decimals) == value for known in known_numbers)
```

## Claude · 2026-11-02 07:25

I've done items 1–4 and the test question now comes back right **10 times out of 10** (it was 0 out of 3 before). The answer is always 9.5 %, 640 / 6704 trains, interval 8.9 %–10.3 %. Nothing is committed.

**How I got there.** I made one small change at a time, re-ran the question 3–5 times, and read the trace of every failed run before the next change. Thinking was ON, which is the default.

| Step | Change | Correct runs |
|---|---|---|
| baseline | no changes (8 turns max) | 0/3 |
| v1 | prompt fixes (a) and (b), station names are in capitals, every `run_python` call starts from scratch, `MAX_TURNS` 8 → 10 | 2/3 |
| v2 | guard reads the French "6 704" as 6704 | 4/5 |
| v3 | one filter example with parentheses | 4/5 |
| v4 | copy the % exactly as `confidence_interval` prints it | 4/5 |
| v5 | call `describe_dataset` first when a station is named; never type numbers from earlier output into the code | 5/5 |
| final | no station names in the prompt examples; an empty reply is no longer accepted as the answer | **10/10** |

The final version also got 4 out of 4 on two other questions, one in English (12.0 %) and one in French (27.7 %), so it isn't tuned to this one question. Most runs take 4 turns and about 45 s. The tests still pass (55).

**Three changes outside the original list:**
- **`guard.py`:** a correct French answer with "6 704" was being blocked, because the guard read it as two numbers, 6 and 704. Telling Qwen not to put spaces in numbers didn't work, so I changed the code: a space before exactly 3 digits now counts as a thousands separator, like the comma already did.
- **`agent.py`:** with thinking on, Qwen sometimes ended a turn with an empty reply. The agent took that as the final answer, and the guard passed it because there were no numbers to check. Now the agent tells the model the reply was empty and keeps going.
- **Station names in the prompt:** my first examples used real station names, and Qwen once copied 'PARIS MONTPARNASSE' into its query instead of PARIS LYON. So the examples no longer name any station.

**For your defense (brick 3):** one wrong answer got past the guard. Qwen typed January's numbers (53 and 548) straight into its code, the tool printed them, and the guard accepted them. That's the limit already noted in `not to forget.md`: the guard checks that a tool printed each number, not that the number answers the question. The v5 prompt rule makes it rarer, but the code can't stop it.

**Needs your decision:** `.gitignore` line 27 (`conversations/`) is meant for the app's saved chats, but it also matches `ai_log/conversations/`. Today's log won't be committed, which matters for R3. Changing it to `/conversations/` would fix it. Do you want me to?

The changed files are `prompts.py`, `agent.py` and `guard.py`. The change-by-change results table is in `ai_log/conversations/2026-11-02-session.md`, and I marked the plan done in `not to forget.md`. For your journal (R3), you'll want to note which prompt lines were rewritten and why; that table has it.

## Claude · 2026-11-02 07:38

`EVIDENCE.md` belongs to the **bricks**, not to any R. In the spec (`Projet_Final_Coding_for_AI_2026-27.pdf`, "Livrables" section) it's listed as a deliverable of the Git repo, next to the README and the tests:

> « EVIDENCE.md : une section par brique, avec ce que vous montrez, les fichiers, et la commande qui le reproduit. »

So it has **5 sections, one per brick** (1, 3, 6, 8, 9). Each section says:
1. **What you show**, e.g. "the guard blocks an answer with a number no tool printed".
2. **The files**, e.g. `guard.py`, `tests/test_guard.py`, a trace.
3. **The command that reproduces it**, e.g. `pytest tests/test_guard.py`.

It matters for the grade because each brick gets **2 points only if it is "working with evidence"**. Code that exists but isn't shown gets 1 point. Across 5 bricks, EVIDENCE.md decides up to 5 of the 10 brick points.

It doesn't cover R1–R4. Those are shown elsewhere:
- **R1:** the README.
- **R2:** the tests.
- **R3:** the `ai_log/` folder.
- **R4:** the oral defense.

There's one overlap: a brick's evidence can be a test. For example, brick 3 needs "a pytest that tries to break it and gets blocked". So `test_guard.py` counts for R2 and is also the evidence for brick 3.

Do you want me to go ahead with the test cases for step 2?

```
"""Brick 8: confidence intervals for punctuality rates."""

from math import sqrt
from statistics import NormalDist


def confidence_interval(successes, total, level=0.95):
    """Wilson confidence interval for a proportion successes / total.

    Wilson is used instead of the simple "p +/- z * sqrt(p(1-p)/n)" interval
    because it stays between 0 and 1 and works when p is close to 0 or 1.
    Returns (rate, low, high), all between 0 and 1.
    """
    if total <= 0:
        raise ValueError("total must be greater than 0")
    if successes < 0 or successes > total:
        raise ValueError("successes must be between 0 and total")

    z = NormalDist().inv_cdf(1 - (1 - level) / 2)  # 1.96 for 95%
    p = successes / total
    denominator = 1 + z**2 / total
    center = (p + z**2 / (2 * total)) / denominator
    margin = z * sqrt(p * (1 - p) / total + z**2 / (4 * total**2)) / denominator
    return p, max(0.0, center - margin), min(1.0, center + margin)
```

## Claude · 2026-11-02 07:46

Here are the test cases I'd write. Nothing is written yet; I'd like your OK first.

**Rules from the spec and the recap:**
- **No model calls in R2 tests.** The recap says "sans appeler le modèle". Checking Qwen's real answers is brick 9.
- **They must pass on a fresh clone.** `data/clean/` is gitignored, so the tests can't read the real CSV. They use tiny hand-made tables instead.
- **Known answers.** Every expected value is written down in advance.

**How they still use real Qwen behaviour.** In the harness tests, a fake `llm.chat` replays real Qwen replies copied from today's traces: the "9,55 %" answer, the "6 704" answer, the empty reply. So the tests are offline, but the inputs are what Qwen actually does.

## 1. `test_guard.py` (R2 + brick 3 evidence)
- **Reading numbers:**
  - "12,792" → 12792
  - "6 704" (normal and no-break space) → 6704
  - "9,5 %" → 9.5
  - "en 2024 640 trains" → 2024 and 640, not merged
  - no number → `[]`
- **Rounding:** the tool printed 0.84567, so 0.85 and 0.846 pass but 0.84 is blocked.
- **Tries to break the rule, using real Qwen answers:**
  - "9,55 %" and "9,6 %" are blocked when the tool printed "0.0955 (9.5 %)".
  - An invented "about 700 trains" is blocked.
  - The correct 11-02 answer passes, and so does "6 704".
  - Numbers from the question ("2024") and from `DATA_FACTS` ("2018") are allowed.
- **Known limit, written as a test:** "9 %" passes because 8.9 rounds to 9. It's useful to show at the defense.
- **`check_code`:** `df.to_csv(...)`, `open(`, `import os` and `subprocess` are blocked; normal pandas code is allowed.

## 2. `test_stats.py` (R2 + brick 8)
- 50/100 → [0.4038, 0.5962], the textbook Wilson value.
- 640/6704 → 0.0955, [0.0887, 0.1027].
- **Edge cases:**
  - 0/10 → low = 0 (allowing a 1e-17 float error)
  - 10/10 → high = 1
  - always 0 ≤ low ≤ rate ≤ high ≤ 1
- A 99 % interval is wider than a 95 % one.
- total = 0, late > total and late < 0 raise `ValueError`.

## 3. `test_tools.py` (R2 + brick 1)
These use a 3-row fixture CSV, with `tools.CLEAN_CSV` pointed at it.
- **`run_python`:**
  - `print(len(df))` → "3"
  - no `print` → the "(no output…)" hint
  - bad code → "Error" plus the `NameError` line
  - `df.to_csv(...)` → refused, and no file is created
  - endless loop → timeout error (timeout set to 1 s in the test)
  - very long output → cut at 4000 characters
- **`confidence_interval(640, 6704)`** → contains "9.5 %" and "[8.9 %, 10.3 %]"; total 0 → "Error…", no crash.
- **`describe_dataset`** on the fixture → "Rows: 3", the month range, the stations.
- **`call_tool`:** an unknown tool or wrong arguments return error text, not a crash. `TOOL_SCHEMAS` names match `TOOLS`.

## 4. `test_cleaning.py` (R2 + brick 6)
There's one small table per step, built from the anomalies we found in the raw file.
- **Rows removed:** 0 planned trains, and more cancellations than planned trains (2 rows).
- **`sup_30` set to missing:** when it's negative or smaller than `sup_60`; normal rows are kept.
- **Negative averages:** a late-trains-only average goes to missing, but the all-trains average stays (it can be negative because of early trains).
- **`sup15` average set to missing:** below 15, or a copy of the all-trains value; 20 is kept.
- **Causes:** all 6 at 0 → missing.
- **Merge:** National with 100 trains plus International with 50 → counts added, averages weighted by trains, service "International/National".
- **SNCF rule boundaries:** 89 min → 5, 90 → 10, 180 → 10, 181 → 15.
- **Derived columns:** `nb_circules` = planned − cancelled; 0 trains ran → rate is missing, no division error.
- **Dates:** "2024-07" → 2024, 7; a bad date raises an error.

## 5. New: `test_agent.py` (the harness, with a fake Qwen)
- **Normal path:** tool call → answer → status `ok`, one line in a temporary `traces.jsonl`.
- **The rule from end to end:** fake Qwen answers "9,55 %" twice → status `rejected`, the user sees "Answer blocked…", and the retry message named 9.55. This shows the code blocks it whatever the model says.
- **Retry that works:** wrong first, corrected second → `ok`, `retried = True`.
- **Empty reply (today's bug):** an empty reply makes the agent send its "Your reply was empty" message and continue, so the empty reply is never taken as the answer.
- **Turn limit:** a model that only ever calls tools → `max_turns`.
- **Broken JSON arguments:** an error message goes back to the model, no crash.

**Two small setup changes:**
- `pytest.ini`: `pythonpath = src scripts`, so the tests can import `clean_data`.
- No `conftest.py`: each file builds its own small data, which is easier to explain.

Should I go with all 5 files? I'd write them one at a time and show you each one before the next.

## Claude · 2026-11-02 07:53

By "fake Qwen" I mean a small function in the test that **takes the place of `llm.chat()`** and returns replies we wrote in advance, instead of asking the real model.

**The normal chain:**
```
agent.py  ──llm.chat(messages)──►  llm.py  ──HTTP──►  Ollama (Qwen)
          ◄──── Qwen's reply ─────
```

**The chain during the test:**
```
agent.py  ──llm.chat(messages)──►  fake_chat()   (in the test file)
          ◄── a reply we wrote ──
```

`agent.py` doesn't notice the difference. It gets a reply in the same format, so the loop, the tools, the guard, the retry and the trace all run for real. Only Qwen is swapped out.

**What it looks like:**
```python
def test_invented_number_is_blocked(monkeypatch, tmp_path):
    # Qwen's two replies, copied from a real trace of 2026-11-02
    replies = [
        tool_call("confidence_interval", {"late": 640, "total": 6704}),
        answer("Le taux est de 9,55 %."),   # Qwen converts by itself
        answer("Le taux est de 9,55 %."),   # same mistake after the retry
    ]
    def fake_chat(messages, tools=None, think=False):
        return replies.pop(0)               # next reply in the list

    monkeypatch.setattr(agent.llm, "chat", fake_chat)   # swap llm.chat for this test only
    result = agent.run_agent("Taux de retard ?", trace_file=tmp_path / "t.jsonl")

    assert result["status"] == "rejected"
    assert "9.55" in result["answer"]
```

`monkeypatch` is built into pytest. It replaces `llm.chat` during this one test and puts the real one back afterwards.

**Why do it this way:**
1. **R2 forbids calling the model.** The recap says "sans appeler le modèle".
2. **Qwen's answers change from run to run.** A test needs the same answer every time to have a known result. With fake replies, the test always gives the same result.
3. **We choose the tricky cases**, like an empty reply, a made-up number or broken JSON. Real Qwen does these only sometimes.
4. **It's fast and runs anywhere.** No Ollama and no GPU needed, so it also passes on a fresh clone.

**What it doesn't test:** whether real Qwen answers correctly. That's brick 9, the benchmark, which runs the real model on 10+ questions.

This is the same idea `tests/test_app.py` already uses with its `fake_agent`. There the whole agent is faked to test the display; here only the model is faked to test the agent.

Should I go ahead with the 5 files, one at a time?

## Claude · 2026-11-02 07:56

I checked again, and I overstated it: **the official spec doesn't forbid calling the model in R2.** The words "sans appeler le modèle" are only in the recap.

**1. Official spec, `Projet_Final_Coding_for_AI_2026-27.pdf`, section 4:**

> **R2 — Il est vérifié** : « Des tests automatiques à réponse connue pour votre propre code (pytest, ou un vérificateur comme le verifier.py du kit). Ils passent sur un clone tout neuf. »

It sets three conditions: the answers are known in advance, the tests cover **your own code**, and they pass on a fresh clone. It doesn't mention the model at all.

**2. Recap, `Récapitulatif — Projet final Coding for AI & Data Science.pdf`, page 1:**

> « R2 — Il est vérifié : des tests pytest sur tout mon propre code (nettoyage, outils, garde-fou, mémoire…), avec réponse connue à l'avance, **sans appeler le modèle**. Vérifier les réponses de l'agent, c'est la brique 9 (optionnelle). »

The recap is dated Oct 31, 2026 and written in the first person ("mon propre code", "je lance la démo"). It looks like a summary someone wrote, not the teachers' document. I can't tell who wrote it or where the extra words come from; you'll know better than me. If it's your own summary, the "sans appeler le modèle" part may be an interpretation, not a rule.

**Faking Qwen is still the right choice, because the official wording requires it in practice:**
- **"À réponse connue":** Qwen gives different answers from run to run (0/3 to 10/10 today), so a test that calls it has no fixed known answer.
- **"Votre propre code":** the code under test is the agent loop and the guard, not Qwen.
- **"Ils passent sur un clone tout neuf":** a test that needs Ollama and a 3 GB model running fails on a fresh clone without them.

So the plan stays the same, but the right reason to give at the defense is "the official R2 asks for known answers and a fresh clone, so the model is replaced". Not "the spec forbids calling the model".

Should I go ahead with the 5 test files on that basis?

I checked both points against the official spec (`Projet_Final_Coding_for_AI_2026-27.pdf`) and the architecture diagram (`ai-system-architecture.pdf`). Neither recap "rule" is in them, and neither one led us wrong. We don't need to change anything we've built.

## 1. "Don't call the model" (R2)

**What the spec says (section 4):**
> « R2 — Il est vérifié : Des tests automatiques à réponse connue pour votre propre code (pytest, ou un vérificateur comme le verifier.py du kit). Ils passent sur un clone tout neuf. »

The diagram's R2 box says the same thing: *"pytest / tests/ · python -m pytest · Known-answer tests pass from a fresh clone"*.

**Fixed:** my memory no longer says "no model calls". It quotes the spec's R2 wording and marks the recap as unofficial, never to be cited.

**How the test plan matches each condition:**

| What the spec asks | Our plan |
|---|---|
| "votre propre code" | Every file tests our own code: guard, stats, tools, cleaning, agent loop ✅ |
| "à réponse connue" | Every expected value is written in advance; Qwen is replaced by replies copied from real traces, so the result is the same every run ✅ |
| "clone tout neuf" | Small hand-made data, no `data/clean/`, no Ollama ✅ |
| Brick 3: « un test qui essaie d'enfreindre la règle et se fait bloquer » | `test_guard.py` (real wrong Qwen answers like "9,55 %", `df.to_csv`) and `test_agent.py` (blocked from end to end) ✅ This file is the diagram's `tests/test_guardrails.py` |
| Diagram: R2 pytest and B9 `benchmark.py` are in the same "verification runner" but are two separate things | Checking real Qwen answers goes in brick 9, not in pytest ✅ |

**Three changes to the plan so it matches what the teachers ask for:**
1. **More edge cases.** Section 8 says: « Que se passe-t-il dans le cas limite (liste vide, valeur manquante, une seule valeur) ? » I'll add a test for each:
   - **Empty:** an answer with no numbers, an empty list of tool outputs, an empty reply from the model.
   - **Missing value:** NaN in a cleaning step, and in `weighted_mean` (including when all weights are 0).
   - **Single value:** a 1-row table, and an interval for 1 late train out of 1.
2. **`tests/fixtures/`.** The diagram puts test data there (*"pytest fixtures / tests/fixtures/"*), so the small CSV goes there instead of inside the test file.
3. **What the tests don't prove.** `test_tools`, `test_stats` and `test_cleaning` count for R2 only. Bricks 1, 6 and 8 each need their own evidence:
   - **Brick 1:** a real Qwen trace.
   - **Brick 6:** the cleaning log and a rule with its source named.
   - **Brick 8:** the sentence on what the data can't conclude.

## 2. "Clean the data yourself, not the model" (brick 6)

That sentence isn't in the spec either. What brick 6 asks:
> « De vraies données (une API, du web scraping, des données ouvertes, votre propre collecte) avec un journal de nettoyage (lignes retirées, valeurs converties, libellés corrigés) et au moins une règle que le fichier ne dit pas, obtenue auprès d'une personne ou dans un document : dites de qui. »

And section 8 says: « vous décidez quoi construire, quoi nettoyer et quoi mesurer ; l'IA vous aide à l'écrire et à le lancer ».

**What we already do matches it:**
- Real open SNCF data.
- `clean_data.py` cleans it before Qwen ever sees it; the diagram's B6 box is "pandas / clean.py".
- The cleaning log is in `data/cleaning_log.csv`.
- You decided each cleaning step.

The only missing piece is the rule from a named source, which is step 3 of the plan.

**Two other things the diagram shows:**
- **Where the rule goes.** The diagram names `data/RULES.md` (*"B6 pandas / clean.py + data/RULES.md"*). I suggest writing the SNCF rule there instead of `docs/sncf_rule.md`; it isn't gitignored.
- **Traces in git.** The diagram lists `traces.jsonl` as an evidence file (*"Calls + results: traces.jsonl"*), but our `traces/` folder is gitignored. Brick 1 will need a committed trace, from `benchmark/traces/` or a trace file we choose to commit. We can decide when we write EVIDENCE.md.

The diagram's other file names (`harness.py`, `guardrails.py`, `policy.json`) are examples. The spec doesn't require them, so our names (`agent.py`, `guard.py`) are fine.

Should I start writing the tests, one file at a time with those three changes, beginning with `test_guard.py`? And do you agree to move the SNCF rule to `data/RULES.md`?

From the project folder, activate the venv and run it:

```bash
cd "/mnt/shared-os-space/shared_os_space/School/PGE3/Coding with AI/final_exam/tgv-delay-analyst"
source .venv/bin/activate
python -m pytest tests/test_guard.py -v
```

`-v` prints every test by name with PASSED or FAILED, so you can see all 41. Without `-v` you only get dots.

**A few useful variants:**

```bash
python -m pytest tests/test_guard.py -k "blocked" -v     # only tests whose name contains "blocked"
python -m pytest "tests/test_guard.py::test_rate_converted_by_the_model_is_blocked" -v   # one single test
python -m pytest tests/test_guard.py -q                 # short output: dots + summary
```

**Make sure the tests can actually fail.** A test that always passes proves nothing. Break the guard on purpose and watch:

1. Open `src/tgv_analyst/guard.py`. In `THOUSANDS_PATTERN`, replace `[, \u00a0\u202f]` with `,`, which undoes today's fix for "6 704".
2. Run `python -m pytest tests/test_guard.py -q`. Several tests should fail, including `test_correct_answer_with_french_thousands_passes`. The red output shows the number the guard wrongly rejected.
3. Put the line back: `git checkout src/tgv_analyst/guard.py` restores it. Run the tests again and you should get 41 passed.

You can do the same with `check_code`. Remove `"to_csv",` from `FORBIDDEN_CODE`, and `test_forbidden_code_is_blocked` fails on the `df.to_csv('copy.csv')` case. Restore it with the same `git checkout` command.

This is also a good thing to show at the defense: break the rule, watch the test go red, restore it. Tell me when you're done and I'll move on to `test_stats.py`.

```
 "content": "\"\"\"Tests for stats.py: the Wilson confidence interval (R2, used by brick 8).\n\nThe expected values do not come from running stats.py: they are a published\nvalue, formulas worked out by hand for the edge cases, and the simple\n\"p +/- 1.96 * sqrt(p(1-p)/n)\" interval, which Wilson matches when n is large.\n\"\"\"\n\nfrom math import sqrt\n\nimport pytest\n\nfrom tgv_analyst.stats import confidence_interval\n\nZ95 = 1.959964  # z for a 95% interval\n\n\n# --- Known values -----------------------------------------------------------\n\ndef test_textbook_value_50_out_of_100():\n    \"\"\"Published Wilson 95% interval for 50 / 100: [0.4038, 0.5962].\"\"\"\n    rate, low, high = confidence_interval(50, 100)\n    assert rate == 0.5\n    assert low == pytest.approx(0.4038, abs=1e-4)\n    assert high == pytest.approx(0.5962, abs=1e-4)\n\n\ndef test_paris_lyon_marseille_2024():\n    \"\"\"The benchmark question: 640 late trains out of 6704 -> 9.5 %, [8.9 %, 10.3 %].\"\"\"\n    rate, low, high = confidence_interval(640, 6704)\n    assert rate == pytest.approx(0.0955, abs=1e-4)\n    assert low == pytest.approx(0.0887, abs=1e-4)\n    assert high == pytest.approx(0.1027, abs=1e-4)\n\n\ndef test_large_sample_matches_the_simple_interval():\n    \"\"\"With 1,000,000 trains, Wilson and p +/- z*sqrt(p(1-p)/n) give the same bounds.\"\"\"\n    n, late = 1_000_000, 500_000\n    p = late / n\n    margin = Z95 * sqrt(p * (1 - p) / n)\n    rate, low, high = confidence_interval(late, n)\n    assert low == pytest.approx(p - margin, abs=1e-6)\n    assert high == pytest.approx(p + margin, abs=1e-6)\n\n\n# --- Edge cases (spec section 8: empty, missing, single value) --------------\n\ndef test_no_late_train():\n    \"\"\"0 late out of 10: the rate is 0 but the interval is not [0, 0].\n\n    By hand, for 0 successes the upper bound is z^2 / (n + z^2).\n    \"\"\"\n    rate, low, high = confidence_interval(0, 10)\n    assert rate == 0.0\n    assert low == pytest.approx(0.0, abs=1e-12)  # 2.8e-17 because of float rounding\n    assert high == pytest.approx(Z95**2 / (10 + Z95**2), abs=1e-6)  # 0.2775\n\n\ndef test_all_trains_late():\n    \"\"\"10 late out of 10: the upper bound is exactly 1, never above.\"\"\"\n    rate, low, high = confidence_interval(10, 10)\n    assert rate == 1.0\n    assert high == 1.0\n    assert low == pytest.approx(10 / (10 + Z95**2), abs=1e-6)  # 0.7225, mirror of the case above\n\n\ndef test_single_train_late():\n    \"\"\"A single train, and it was late: the interval is very wide.\n\n    By hand, for 1 success out of 1 the lower bound is 1 / (1 + z^2).\n    \"\"\"\n    rate, low, high = confidence_interval(1, 1)\n    assert rate == 1.0\n    assert low == pytest.approx(1 / (1 + Z95**2), abs=1e-6)  # 0.2065\n    assert high == 1.0\n\n\ndef test_single_train_on_time():\n    rate, low, high = confidence_interva
```

```
0 late out of 10  →  rate = 0.0, low = 0.0000000000000000278
```

The lower bound comes out slightly **above** the rate, because of float rounding in `center - margin`. The existing `max(0.0, …)` only stops a bound from going below 0, so it doesn't catch this. It's harmless in practice: `confidence_interval` prints "0.0000", so Qwen and users never see it. But "low ≤ rate" is mathematically always true, and the code breaks it.

**Two ways to handle it:**
1. **Fix `stats.py` (recommended).** It's a one-line change so the bounds can never cross the rate:
   ```python
   return p, min(p, max(0.0, center - margin)), max(p, min(1.0, center + margin))
   ```
   The test stays strict and documents the fix.
2. **Leave `stats.py` as it is** and loosen the test to allow a 1e-12 tolerance, noting it as a float limit.

Which one do you want? Option 1 also makes a good R3 journal entry: "test found a float bug, corrected by hand".

## Claude · 2026-11-02 08:29

**1. Am I updating the conversation file?** Yes. After each exchange I add an entry to `ai_log/conversations/2026-11-02-session.md`: your message word for word, then a summary of what I answered and did. It has 14 entries now, including this question.

It is a **summary**, though, not a full export. R3 asks for « l'export de vos conversations », and a summary may count for less. Claude Code keeps the **full transcripts** of every session on your machine as `.jsonl` files (in `~/.claude/projects/...final-exam/`). There is one for every session so far, including yesterday afternoon's (`a67830d5…`), which was never logged. Later I can convert those into readable `.md` files in `ai_log/conversations/`. That would give real exports and fill the gap from yesterday afternoon. I'd check them for personal data first.

**2. What is a fixture?** It's **test data or setup prepared in advance, with known content**, so a test always starts from the same state. The word covers two things, and we'll use both:

- **A fixture file:** a small data file kept in the repo just for tests. Here it would be `tests/fixtures/tgv_small.csv`, about 3 hand-written rows with the same columns as the clean data. The real `data/clean/tgv_clean.csv` isn't in git (it's built by the scripts), so on a fresh clone the tests would have nothing to read without this file. Because we write the rows ourselves, we know the answers: 3 rows, 2 stations, a total of 150 trains, and so on.

- **A pytest fixture function:** a function marked `@pytest.fixture` that prepares something before each test that asks for it. For example:
  ```python
  @pytest.fixture
  def small_data(monkeypatch):
      # the tools read the small file instead of the real dataset
      monkeypatch.setattr(tools, "CLEAN_CSV", FIXTURES / "tgv_small.csv")

  def test_run_python_counts_rows(small_data):      # pytest runs small_data() first
      assert tools.run_python("print(len(df))").strip() == "3"
  ```
  A test lists the fixture as a parameter, and pytest runs it first. `monkeypatch` is itself a fixture built into pytest; that's why we could use it in the fake-Qwen tests without writing anything.

The architecture diagram also mentions it: *"Known answers and project rules — pytest fixtures / tests/fixtures/"*.

Should I write `tests/fixtures/tgv_small.csv` and `test_tools.py` now?

| date | route | ran | late |

| 2024-02 | PARIS LYON → MARSEILLE ST CHARLES | 50 | 5 (`sup_30` is missing) |
| 2024-01 | LYON PART DIEU → MARSEILLE ST CHARLES | 40 | 6 |

A fixture function, `small_data`, points the tools at this file instead of the real dataset. That's why the tests also pass on a fresh clone.

**What the tests cover:**

| Tool | Cases |
|---|---|
| `run_python`: normal results | 3 rows; PARIS LYON 2024 → "15 150"; the missing value stays missing (not read as 0); an empty filter gives "0 0" |
| `run_python`: what Qwen gets back when it goes wrong | The no-print hint; `NameError` (Qwen's real mistake of calling `confidence_interval` inside `run_python`); `SyntaxError`; output printed before an error is kept; variables don't carry over to the next call; deleting rows of `df` doesn't change the file; `df.to_csv` is refused and no file is created; an endless loop stops after 1 s; output is cut at 4000 characters |
| `describe_dataset` | Rows, month range, routes, stations sorted with no repeats, every column with its meaning |
| `confidence_interval` (the tool) | The exact text "rate: 0.0955 (9.5 %)…"; wrong counts give an error message, not a crash |
| `call_tool` | Runs the right tool; an unknown tool or wrong arguments return error text; the tool names sent to the model match the ones the code knows |

On the first run, one test failed because of my own mistake: the column list in `tools.py` is in a different order than the CSV. I changed that test to compare the names only, ignoring order.

The suite now takes 13 s instead of 7 s, because each `run_python` test starts a separate Python process, like the real tool does.

`plan.md` and the session log are updated. Next is `test_cleaning.py`. It needs `scripts` added to `pythonpath` in `pytest.ini` so the tests can import `clean_data`. Should I go ahead?

## Claude · 2026-11-02 13:45

I've re-read `scripts/clean_data.py`, which has 9 steps plus `load_raw`, `weighted_mean` and `late_threshold`, and `data/cleaning_log.csv`. Here's the plan. Nothing is written yet.

## Setup
- **`pytest.ini`:** change to `pythonpath = src scripts` so the tests can `import clean_data`. It's a script, not part of the package.
- **New fixture `tests/fixtures/tgv_raw_small.csv`:** a few rows in the **raw** SNCF format, with `;` separators, the BOM at the start, the 3 comment columns, and a comment with a line break inside quotes. The anomalies are the real ones from our profiling, so the test data has the same problems as the real file.
- Each step test builds a 2–4 row table directly in the test, so the input and expected output sit side by side.

## One test (or a few) per step

| Step | Input | Expected |
|---|---|---|
| `load_raw` | the raw fixture | first column is `date`, not `\ufeffdate` (BOM removed); the comment with a line break stays one row |
| `drop_comment_columns` | table with the 3 comment columns | they're gone, all other columns kept, log says 0 rows |
| `remove_impossible_rows` | normal row, 0 planned, more cancellations than planned | 1 row left, log says 2 |
| `fix_broken_sup30` | `sup_30` negative, `sup_30 < sup_60`, normal row | first two set to missing, third kept, log 2 |
| `fix_negative_delays` | negative **late-trains-only** average, negative **all-trains** average | the first goes to missing, the second stays (early trains make it negative), log 1 |
| `fix_copied_sup15_average` | 12 (below 15), exact copy of the all-trains average, 20 | 12 and the copy go to missing, 20 kept, log 2 |
| `fix_empty_causes` | 6 causes all 0, causes adding up to 100 | first row set to missing, second kept |
| `merge_service_duplicates` | same route and month as National (100 trains) + International (50 trains), plus another route | one row: counts added, averages weighted by trains (computed by hand in the test), service "International/National"; the other route is untouched; log 2 |
| `convert_dates` | "2024-07" | year 2024, month 7; "07/2024" raises an error (fails loudly) |
| `add_derived_columns` | 100 planned, 10 cancelled, 9 late | `nb_circules` 90, rate 0.1, cancellation rate 0.1; 0 trains ran → missing rate, no division by 0 |

## Helpers and the SNCF rule
- **`weighted_mean`:** normal case computed by hand; a missing value gives a missing result; all weights 0 falls back to a simple mean. These are the spec's section 8 edge cases.
- **`late_threshold`** (the brick 6 rule): 89 min → 5, 90 → 10, 180 → 10, 181 → 15.

  ⚠️ These boundaries test what the code **does now**. At step 3 (`data/RULES.md`) we'll check them against SNCF's document. If the source says "≤ 90" instead of "< 90", we change the code and the test together.

## The whole pipeline
- **`clean()` on the raw fixture:**
  - exact final row count
  - the log has 9 entries in the right order, each with the expected row count
  - the final columns are exactly the 29 the tools expect, the same names as `tools.COLUMN_DESCRIPTIONS`; this links brick 6 to brick 1
- **A table with nothing wrong:** every fix step logs 0 rows and the data comes out unchanged.

**What it doesn't test:** the counts on the real file (73, 354, 2183…). That file isn't in git, so a test on it would fail on a fresh clone. Those numbers belong in `data/cleaning_log.csv`, which is committed, as brick 6 evidence.

That comes to about 25 tests. Should I go ahead?

**What the tests cover:**
- **`load_raw`:** the BOM is removed and the 2-line comment stays one row.

The real clean file already stores this column as decimals.

## Claude · 2026-11-02 14:00

I've re-read `agent.py`. Here's the plan for `tests/test_agent.py`. Nothing is written yet.

## How it works
- **Fake Qwen:** a small helper replaces `llm.chat` with a list of replies written in advance, handed back one per turn. Each reply has the exact shape Ollama sends: `message` (content and/or tool_calls), `usage`, `seconds`.
- **Three helpers to build those replies:** `tool_call("confidence_interval", {...})`, `answer("Le taux est de 9,5 %…")` and `empty()`.
- **It records what the agent sends.** The fake keeps every `messages` list it receives, so we can check what the agent sent to the model: the system prompt, the tool results, the retry message.
- **Real tools, safe files.** The tools really run, on the 3-row fixture via the same `small_data` trick as `test_tools`. `confidence_interval` needs no data at all. Traces are written to a temporary file, never to the real `traces/traces.jsonl`.
- **Real Qwen wording.** The answers are copied from today's real traces (the "9,55 %", "9,6 %" and "6 704" answers).

## Test cases

| # | Scenario (fake Qwen replies) | Expected |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Normal path:** `confidence_interval(640, 6704)` → real correct answer | status `ok`; the answer is the model's text; 2 turns; 1 JSON line in the trace file with question, status, steps and tokens added up |
| 2 | **What the model receives:** same run | 1st message = system prompt, 2nd = the question; the tool result goes back with the right `tool_call_id` |
| 3 | **With `run_python`** on the fixture: prints "15 150" → `confidence_interval(15, 150)` → answer | `ok`; numbers checked against both tool outputs |
| 4 | **Brick 3 from end to end:** "9,55 %" twice | status `rejected`; the user sees "Answer blocked: … 9.55"; the retry message sent to the model names 9.55; the trace keeps the blocked `model_answer` |
| 5 | **Retry that works:** "9,6 %" then "9,5 %" | `ok`, `retried = True` |
| 6 | **Empty reply (today's bug):** empty → answer | `ok`; the agent sent "Your reply was empty…"; the empty reply is never the final answer |
| 7 | **Only empty replies** | `max_turns`, never `ok` (before today's fix this was a false "ok") |
| 8 | **Turn limit:** a model that only ever calls tools, with `max_turns=3` | `max_turns`; exactly 3 model calls; message "did not finish within 3 turns" |
| 9 | **Broken JSON arguments** | "Error: arguments are not valid JSON." goes back to the model, the loop goes on, no crash |
| 10 | **Unknown tool** | an error goes back to the model, the loop goes on |
| 11 | **Forbidden code:** `run_python("df.to_csv(...)")` | the refusal goes back to the model; no file is created |
| 12 | **Greeting**, no tool and no number | `ok` with no tool call |
| 13 | **Events for the display:** a collector passed as `on_event` | the events come in order (Thinking → ToolCall → ToolResult → GuardVerdict → FinalAnswer); `FinalAnswer.sources` links "9,5" to tool call 1 |
| 14 | **Thinking setting** | `think=False` reaches `llm.chat` |

## Small helpers in `agent.py`
- **`parse_arguments`:** a dict is kept as is; valid JSON text is decoded; broken JSON or a JSON list gives an error message. Empty text gives `{}`.
- **`error_line`:** returns the last line of a traceback, or None when the call worked.
- **`count_rows`:** 3 for the fixture; None for a file that doesn't exist.
- **`check_startup`:** with fake `ping` and data paths:
  - everything fine → both OK
  - server down → `server_ok False`
  - data missing → `data_ok False`
  - the guard is always reported as on

## What it doesn't test
Whether the real Qwen answers well. That's brick 9, the benchmark.

This file is also the **end-to-end brick 3 evidence**. `test_guard.py` checks the guard function by itself; test 4 shows that the agent really blocks the answer, whatever the model writes.

That comes to about 25 tests. Should I go ahead?

## Claude · 2026-11-02 14:04

**This test found a real bug, and I introduced it this morning.** When I taught the guard to read the French "6 704" as 6704, I made it read **every** "number space 3 digits" as one number, **tool outputs included**:

```
run_python printed:   15 150        (print(late, total): two numbers)
the guard now reads:  15150         (one number)
→ the correct answer "15 sur 150" is blocked
```

`print(a, b)` is exactly what Qwen writes all the time, so this matters. The tuning runs this morning passed only because Qwen happened to print each number on its own line.

**Proposed fix (in `guard.py`):** a space can be a thousands separator in the **model's answer**, where French typography writes "6 704", but never in a **tool output**, because Python's `print` never writes numbers that way. In practice:
- `extract_numbers(text, spaces=True)` gets a new parameter.
- `check_answer` reads the tool outputs with `spaces=False`, so "15 150" stays 15 and 150. The answer is still read with spaces allowed, so "6 704" still passes.
- `agent.py` reads tool outputs the same way in its two places: `find_sources` and the numbers shown in the tool-result panel.

**Tests to add in `test_guard.py`:**
- the tool printed "15 150" and the answer "15 sur 150" passes
- the tool printed "6704" and the answer "6 704" still passes

That's about 10 changed lines. It's a change to the brick 3 code, so I'd rather you OK it. Should I apply it?

```
"""Brick 3: reject any answer containing a number that no tool computed.

This rule is enforced by code, not by the prompt: the agent calls
check_answer() before showing an answer, whatever the model says.
"""

import re

# A comma or a space followed by exactly 3 digits is a thousands separator:
# 12,792 or 1,234,567.5, and the French 6 704 (normal or no-break space).
THOUSANDS_PATTERN = re.compile(r"\d{1,3}(?:[, \u00a0\u202f]\d{3})+(?:\.\d+)?(?!\d)")
# Matches 12,792 (thousands) first, otherwise 12, 12.5 and 12,5 (French decimal comma).
# The sign is ignored.
NUMBER_PATTERN = re.compile(THOUSANDS_PATTERN.pattern + r"|\d+(?:[.,]\d+)?")


def extract_numbers(text):
    """Return the numbers written in a text, as strings with a "." decimal point.

    "12,792" and "12 792" -> "12792" (thousands separator), "12,5" -> "12.5" (decimal comma).
    Limit: "12,500" is always read as twelve thousand five hundred, never as 12.5.
    """
    numbers = []
    for match in NUMBER_PATTERN.findall(text):
        if THOUSANDS_PATTERN.fullmatch(match):
            numbers.append(re.sub(r"[, \u00a0\u202f]", "", match))
        else:
            numbers.append(match.replace(",", "."))
    return numbers


def is_supported(number, known_numbers):
    """True if `number` equals a known number rounded to the same decimals.

    Example: the tool printed 0.84567, so the answer may say 0.85 or 0.846,
    but not 0.84.
    """
    decimals = len(number.split(".")[1]) if "." in number else 0
    valu
```

```
    "12,792" and "12 792" -> "12792" (thousands separator), "12,5" -> "12.5" (decimal comma).
    Limit: "12,500" is always read as twelve thousand five hundred, never as 12.5.

'''# In text written by the model or a person, a comma or a space followed by exactly
# 3 digits is a thousands separator: 12,792 or 1,234,567.5, and the French 6 704
# (normal or no-break space).
THOUSANDS_PATTERN = re.compile(r"\\d{1,3}(?:[, \\u00a0\\u202f]\\d{3})+(?:\\.\\d+)?(?!\\d)")
# Matches 12,792 (thousands) first, otherwise 12, 12.5 and 12,5 (French decimal comma).
# The sign is ignored.
NUMBER_PATTERN = re.compile(THOUSANDS_PATTERN.pattern + r"|\\d+(?:[.,]\\d+)?")

# In tool output, a space is never a thousands separator: print(15, 150) gives
# "15 150", two numbers. Only the comma counts (Python prints f"{n:,}" as 12,792).
TOOL_THOUSANDS_PATTERN = re.compile(r"\\d{1,3}(?:,\\d{3})+(?:\\.\\d+)?(?!\\d)")
TOOL_NUMBER_PATTERN = re.compile(TOOL_THOUSANDS_PATTERN.pattern + r"|\\d+(?:[.,]\\d+)?")


def extract_numbers(text, spaces=True):
    """Return the numbers written in a text, as strings with a "." decimal point.

    "12,792" and "12 792" -> "12792" (thousands separator), "12,5" -> "12.5" (decimal comma).
    spaces=False for tool output: "15 150" -> "15", "150".
    Limit: "12,500" is always read as twelve thousand five hundred, never as 12.5.
    """
    if spaces:
        thousands, number = THOUSANDS_PATTERN, NUMBER_PATTERN
    else:
        thousands, number = TOOL_THOUSANDS_PATTERN, TOOL_NUMBER_PATTERN
    numbers = []
    for match in number.findall(text):
        if thousands.fullmatch(match):'''),
```

## Claude · 2026-11-02 14:10

I applied the guard fix, and `test_agent.py` is finished. That completes the R2 tests: **203 pass**. Nothing is committed yet.

**The fix:**
- **`guard.py`:** a space now counts as a thousands separator only in text written by the model or a person, so "6 704" in an answer still passes. In tool output, only a comma counts, so `print(late, total)` → "15 150" is read as two numbers, 15 and 150. In code, `extract_numbers` takes a new `spaces=` setting, and tool output is read with `spaces=False`.
- **`agent.py`:** reads tool output the same way in its two places: matching each number in the answer to the tool call that printed it, and the numbers shown in the tool-result panel.
- **`test_guard.py`:** 5 new tests, including the exact case that failed ("15 150" printed, answer "15 sur 150").
- **Live check with real Qwen** on the test question: correct (9.5 %, [8.9 %, 10.3 %]), 6 turns, 60 s.

**What `test_agent.py` covers (29 tests):**
- the normal path and the trace file
- the exact messages the model receives
- `run_python` on the fixture
- **brick 3 from end to end:** fake Qwen writes "9,55 %" twice → answer blocked, the retry message names 9.55, and the trace keeps the blocked answer
- a retry that works, and an answer from memory being blocked
- empty replies, the turn limit, broken JSON, an unknown tool, forbidden code
- the events sent to the display
- the small helpers and `check_startup`

**Bugs the R2 tests found today:**

| Bug | Found by | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Wilson lower bound slightly above the rate (0/10) | `test_stats.py` | fixed in `stats.py` |
| Guard reading "15 150" from a tool as 15150 (my mistake this morning) | `test_agent.py` | fixed in `guard.py` |

There was also one mistake in my own test data: an answer quoting 640/6704 when no tool in that fake run had printed them. The guard blocked it, correctly.

These are good examples for the journal and the defense: the tests caught real bugs, including one an AI introduced.

`plan.md` now marks R2 ✅ (still to check on a real fresh clone with the README) and step 2 done; the session log is updated. Next is step 3, `data/RULES.md` (brick 6). Should I commit and push first?

## User · 2026-11-02 14:13

ok. what was the test agent testing: the loop?

## Claude · 2026-11-02 14:13

Yes, mainly **the loop in `agent.py`**, meaning `run_agent()`. That's the code that connects everything:

```
question → model → tool call? ──yes──► run the tool → result back to the model → model again …
                        │
                        no (the model answered)
                        ▼
                  guard checks the numbers → ok? show it : second chance → still wrong? block it
                        ▼
                  save the trace (traces.jsonl)
```

`test_agent.py` replaces only the **model** with the fake Qwen. Everything else in that diagram really runs, so the tests check each decision the loop makes:

| Loop decision | Test |
|---|---|
| Builds the conversation correctly (system prompt, question, tool results linked to their call) | `test_what_the_model_receives` |
| Runs the tool the model asked for and sends the result back | `test_normal_path`, `test_with_run_python_on_the_data` |
| Recognises the final answer and passes it through the guard | `test_normal_path`, `test_greeting_needs_no_tool` |
| Blocks an answer with an unverified number, after one second chance | `test_invented_number_is_blocked_end_to_end`, `test_second_chance_can_fix_the_answer` |
| Doesn't take an empty reply as an answer | `test_empty_reply_is_not_an_answer`, `test_only_empty_replies_never_give_ok` |
| Stops after `MAX_TURNS` | `test_turn_limit` |
| Survives the model's mistakes (broken JSON, unknown tool, forbidden code) without crashing | 3 tests |
| Saves one trace line per question | `test_normal_path`, `test_invented_number_is_blocked_end_to_end` |
| Sends the right events to the display, in order | `test_events_in_order`, `test_blocked_answer_events` |

It also tests the small helpers in `agent.py` (`parse_arguments`, `error_line`, `count_rows`) and `check_startup()`, the check at launch that the server answers and the data exists.

**What the other test files cover:**
- `test_guard.py` tests the guard **function** by itself. `test_agent.py` shows the loop really **uses** it.
- `test_tools.py` tests each tool by itself. `test_agent.py` shows the loop **calls** them and passes the results back.
- Neither tests whether real Qwen answers correctly. That's brick 9.

For the defense: if a teacher picks `agent.py` and asks "how do you know this works?", `test_agent.py` is the answer, one test per branch of the loop.

## Claude · 2026-11-02 14:22

Almost. The 5 files we planned are done, but checking what the tests actually import, 3 pieces of our own code still have **no test**:

| File | What it does | Tested? |
|---|---|---|
| `guard.py`, `stats.py`, `tools.py`, `clean_data.py`, `agent.py` | the core and the bricks | ✅ today |
| `ui.py`, `app.py`, `sidebar.py`, `conversations.py`, `events.py` | display and saved chats | ✅ yesterday's 55 tests |
| **`llm.py`** | builds the request to Ollama, reads the reply | ❌ every test fakes it, so its own code never runs in a test |
| **`cli.py`** | the `tgv` command and its options | ❌ |
| **`scripts/download_data.py`** | downloads the SNCF file | ❌ |

**Do they need tests?** R2 says « tests … pour votre propre code », and the spec doesn't set a percentage. My view:

- **`llm.py`: worth it.** It's short, and it's the part of the harness that talks to the model. We can test it without Ollama by faking the network call (`urllib.request.urlopen`), with the same idea as the fake Qwen but one level lower. That would check that:
  - `think=False` adds `reasoning_effort: "none"` to the request
  - the API key header is sent only if a key is set
  - the reply comes back as `{message, usage, seconds}`
  - "server not running" gives the clear "Is Ollama running?" error, and "model not found" gives the server's message
  - `ping` returns None when the server doesn't answer

  About 8 tests.
- **`cli.py`: small, optional.** Its job is mostly to wire things together. A few tests on the options (`--no-think` → `think=False`, `--simple`, one question vs. the app) with a fake agent would cover it.
- **`download_data.py`: skip it.** It's a single download from the internet, and a test would need the network, which goes against "à réponse connue" and "clone tout neuf". It gets checked when we test the README on a fresh clone.

**R2 also isn't quite finished:** the spec says the tests must pass **on a fresh clone**. We'll verify that for real at step 5, with the README.

So the core is fully tested, but I'd add `test_llm.py` before calling R2 done. `test_cli.py` is your call. What do you want to do?

```
 "content": "\"\"\"Tests for cli.py: the `tgv` command, its options and the line-by-line mode (R2).\n\nThe display, the agent and the startup check are replaced by fakes: these\ntests check what cli.py decides (which function is called, with which\nsetting, what happens on an error), not how things are drawn (test_ui.py)\nor answered (test_agent.py).\n\"\"\"\n\nfrom contextlib import contextmanager\n\nimport pytest\n\nfrom tgv_analyst import cli, ui\nfrom tgv_analyst.events import StartupStatus\n\nALL_FINE = StartupStatus(True, 0.02, True, 12792, \"qwen-test\", \"localhost:11434\", True)\nSERVER_DOWN = StartupStatus(False, None, True, 12792, \"qwen-test\", \"localhost:11434\", True)\nRESULT = {\"status\": \"ok\", \"answer\": \"There are 12792 rows.\", \"trace\": {}}\n\n\nclass FakeDisplay:\n    \"\"\"Records every call cli.py makes, as (method name, arguments).\"\"\"\n\n    def __init__(self, lines=(), **options):\n        self.options = options  # what ui.Display(...) was created with\n        self.lines = list(lines)  # what the user \"types\", then Ctrl-D\n        self.calls = []\n\n    def read_question(self):\n        if not self.lines:\n            raise EOFError  # Ctrl-D\n        line = self.lines.pop(0)\n        if isinstance(line, BaseException):\n            raise line\n        return line\n\n    @contextmanager\n    def checking(self):\n        self.calls.append((\"checking\",))\n        yield\n\n    def names(self):\n        return [call[0] for call in self.calls]\n\n    def errors(self):\n        return [call[1] for call in self.calls if call[0] == \"show_error\"]\n\n    def __getattr__(self, name):\n        # Any other display method (show_result, show_help, ...) just records its call.\n        return lambda *args: self.calls.append((name, *args))\n\n\[email]\ndef fake_agent(monkeypatch):\n    \"\"\"Replaces run_agent. agent.questions lists (question, think); ## What's left, by grade weight
```

1. **Brick 9 benchmark:** 2 pts, nothing exists yet, and it's the most work. We need 10+ questions with known answers, our system against the bare model, at least 3 runs each, mean and spread, and one failure explained from its trace. Each run with real Qwen takes about 1 minute, so roughly 10 × 2 × 3 = 60 runs ≈ 1 hour of running time. It's best to start it early.
2. **README + fresh-clone test:** R1+R2 are worth 3 pts together, and nothing counts if the teacher can't run the project.
3. **`data/RULES.md`:** completes brick 6. It's short, but we need to find SNCF's document and check our 5/10/15 boundaries against it.
4. **`EVIDENCE.md`:** decides the second point of each brick, up to 5 pts. It comes last because it points to everything else: the trace for brick 1, the sentence for brick 8, the benchmark results.
5. **R3:** your `journal.md` (2 pts), plus converting yesterday afternoon's transcript into `ai_log/`.
6. **R4:** rehearse (5 pts).

- the "national" rate (a sum over all routes)
- where the committed brick 1 trace lives

## Claude · 2026-11-02 14:48

Yes, I read it the same way. Brick 8 asks for **two things**:

1. **One** of the three: « Un intervalle de confiance, **ou** une comparaison A/B, **ou** la réplication d'un résultat publié ». The « ou » means one is enough; we don't need all three.
2. **And** « une phrase sur ce que vos données ne permettent pas de conclure ».

| Part | Status |
|---|---|
| 1. Confidence interval | ✅ `stats.py` (Wilson interval), the `confidence_interval` tool, a 95 % interval in every rate answer (e.g. 9.5 %, [8.9 %, 10.3 %]), tested in `test_stats.py` |
| 2. The sentence | ❌ **missing**: it's not in the code, the answers or the docs |

So part 1 is covered; what we're missing is the sentence. In `plan.md` the sentence was only listed under "Evidence", which made it look secondary, but it's half the requirement.

**What the sentence can say, from our data:**
- **Why trains were late:** the data doesn't say. The `prct_cause_*` columns give a share per broad category (external, infrastructure, traffic…) for each route and month, not the cause of a given train. And for 199 route-months the causes are unknown (all zero, set to missing in cleaning).
- **Individual trains:** the data can't say whether **your** train will be late. It's monthly totals per route.
- **The interval itself:** it treats each train as an independent draw, but trains on the same day share the weather, strikes and works. So the real uncertainty is wider than the interval shows.
- **National rates:** they can't be added up from routes. SNCF warns that a train can appear on several routes and not every route is listed (that's our open "national" question).

**Where to put it:**
- **(a) In every answer (recommended):** add a prompt rule asking Qwen to end each rate answer with one short sentence on what the data can't say. The guard won't block it as long as it has no numbers. The teacher would see it live during the demo, which is the strongest evidence.
- **(b) In `EVIDENCE.md` and the README only:** a fixed paragraph. Simpler, but the system itself never says it.
- **Both:** (a) for the demo, (b) as the written version.

A 4B model may forget a rule like (a) or invent limits. A safer version of (a) is to have the `confidence_interval` tool print the sentence itself, right after the interval. Then it's in code and always correct, and Qwen only has to copy it, like the "9.5 %" it already copies.

**Optional, for extra strength:** an A/B comparison (e.g. "is route X really worse than route Y, or is it noise?") would be a second method. It's not required, so I'd leave it unless time allows.

Which do you prefer: the sentence printed by the tool, a prompt rule, or only the written version?

## Claude · 2026-11-02 14:57

I've put your order in `plan.md` as steps 3 to 10. Before step 3, here are your three points.

## 1. "A file where we prove each brick"
That file is **`EVIDENCE.md`**. The spec asks for it by name, one section per brick, so we don't need a separate file. I'll write it the way you describe: for each brick, what it shows, the files, the command to run, and the result the teacher should see. It's step 9, at the end, because it points to everything else.

## 2. The brick 1 trace: what the course says
- **Lecture 1** defines a trace: *"Every session is a trace: the ordered list of steps, tool calls, results and tokens."* The class used `opencode session export` and a `show_tool_calls.py` script that prints it as a table.
- **Pi** is presented only as a minimal harness to compare with opencode; it doesn't define a trace format.
- **Kit, session 1:** the kit's `harness.py` only prints the tool calls to the screen and doesn't save them.
- **The architecture diagram** says *"Calls + results: traces.jsonl"*.

Our `traces/traces.jsonl` already has exactly that content for every question: each step, each tool call with its arguments, the result, the tokens, the time and the guard verdict. So we don't need to build anything. Since `traces/` is gitignored, we only need to **commit one real Qwen trace**, for example in an `evidence/` folder (step 6). If you want it to read like the lecture's table, a small `show_trace.py` script could print it the same way. That's optional.

## 3. The "national" rate: what to decide
**The problem.** Our data has one row per **route** (departure → arrival) per month. If someone asks "what share of all TGVs were late in 2024?", Qwen adds up all routes: 60,218 late out of 439,992 = 13.7 %. That's the number it gave in yesterday's test.

**Why it may be wrong.** Per our notes from yesterday, SNCF says route figures shouldn't be added up into a national rate, because:
- a train serving several stations may be counted on more than one route
- not every route is in the dataset

So 13.7 % is "the rate over the listed routes", not necessarily SNCF's official national rate. I haven't checked this against the SNCF document yet; that happens at step 3.

**The options:**
1. **Allow it, say what it is (recommended).** The answer says it's computed over the routes in the dataset and isn't SNCF's official national figure. This fits naturally in the brick 8 sentence about what the data can't conclude. Simple, honest, and nothing is blocked.
2. **Refuse national questions.** Safe, but a user asking "which year was worst overall?" gets no answer, and a 4B model would have to recognise what counts as "national".
3. **Allow it silently** and only note the limit in the README. Least work, but the user may take 13.7 % as official.

Tell me which option you want. Meanwhile, step 3 (`data/RULES.md`) starts with finding SNCF's methodology document. It should confirm two things: the late-train thresholds (5/10/15 min) and the warning about adding up routes. Can I search the web for it?

## Claude · 2026-11-02 15:48

Brick 8 needs a sentence like *"The data doesn't say why trains were late"* to appear somewhere. The question is **who writes it**. There are two ways.

**Way A: the tool prints it (my recommendation).** Today the `confidence_interval` tool returns:
```
rate: 0.0955 (9.5 %)
95% confidence interval: [0.0887, 0.1027] ([8.9 %, 10.3 %])
```
I'd add a fixed line written in `tools.py`:
```
rate: 0.0955 (9.5 %)
95% confidence interval: [0.0887, 0.1027] ([8.9 %, 10.3 %])
Limits: the data does not say why trains were late, nor whether one given train
will be late; the interval treats trains as independent, so the real uncertainty
is wider; a sum over routes is not SNCF's official national rate.
```
Qwen reads it like the rate, and a prompt rule tells it to end the answer with that limit. The answer would read something like: *"…9.5 %, between 8.9 % and 10.3 %. These data do not say why trains were late."*

- **Pro:** the text is written by us in code, so it's always present and always correct. Qwen only has to copy it, which it already does well with "9.5 %".
- **Pro:** the national-rate warning (your option 1) comes along for free, because Qwen gives a rate with `confidence_interval` every time.
- **Con:** Qwen may still shorten or skip the copy sometimes. Even then, the sentence is in the tool output, which is saved in every trace, so it's always visible.

**Way B: Qwen writes it from a prompt rule.** We'd only add a rule to the system prompt: *"End every rate answer with one sentence on what the data can't conclude."*

- **Pro:** nothing to change in the tools.
- **Con:** Qwen is a small model. It may forget, or invent a wrong limit, like "the data doesn't include weekends", which is false.

**And either way:** the same sentence also goes into `EVIDENCE.md` and the README as the written version for the teacher.

A small detail for Way A: the sentence has no numbers, so the guard never blocks it.

So: Way A, Way B, or only the written version in the docs?

## Claude · 2026-11-02 15:56

**`confidence_interval`**, one of our 3 tools in `tools.py`. It's the one Qwen calls to get a late rate with its 95 % interval (9.5 %, [8.9 %, 10.3 %]).

**Why that one and not the other two:**
- **`confidence_interval`:** Qwen calls it every time it gives a late rate (prompt rule 7). A late rate is exactly when the limits matter: *why* trains were late, a single train, the interval's assumption, the national sum. So the sentence arrives right when it's needed.
- **`run_python`:** it only prints whatever Qwen's code prints. We'd have to add the sentence to every piece of code, even "how many rows are there?", where it makes no sense.
- **`describe_dataset`:** it's about the columns and stations, called before any computing. A limit printed there would be too far from the final answer, and Qwen would forget it.

The change is a few lines at the end of `confidence_interval` in `tools.py`, plus a short prompt rule like "end your answer with the limit printed by confidence_interval". Should I do it?

## Claude · 2026-11-02 16:00

Let me start from the beginning, step by step.

**1. What the teachers ask (brick 8).** When our system gives a number, it must also say what that number **does not** tell you. Example: *"9.5 % of trains were late, but the data doesn't say why."* That second part is the sentence we're missing.

**2. Who writes our answers today.** Qwen writes the final answer. It takes the numbers our tools printed and turns them into a sentence: *"The late rate is 9.5 %, between 8.9 % and 10.3 %."* No limit is mentioned. Nothing in our system tells Qwen to say it.

**3. The obvious fix and its weakness.** We could add a line to the prompt: *"Always say what the data can't conclude."* But Qwen is a small model. It may forget, or make up a wrong limit, like "the data doesn't count weekends", which is false.

**4. The safer fix.** We already have a trick that works with Qwen: **give it the exact text and it copies it.** That's how it copies "9.5 %" correctly. So we write the limit sentence ourselves, in our Python code, and make it appear in front of Qwen, so it only has to copy it.

**5. Where to make it appear.** Qwen calls our tool `confidence_interval` every time it gives a late rate. That tool returns some text to Qwen, today the rate and the interval. We add our limit sentence to that text:

```
rate: 9.5 %
interval: 8.9 % to 10.3 %
Limits: the data does not say why trains were late.   ← new line, written by us
```

Qwen sees it and copies it into its answer: *"…9.5 %, between 8.9 % and 10.3 %. The data does not say why trains were late."*

**In one line:** the teachers want a "what we can't conclude" sentence; Qwen can't be trusted to invent it; so we write it in our code and the tool hands it to Qwen to copy.

Is that clearer?

## Claude · 2026-11-02 16:07

The live test result is clear: **Qwen copied the limits in 0 of 5 answers.** All 5 answers are correct and passed the guard, but every one stopped after the interval, even though the tool printed the limits and rule 7 asked for them. The 2 "national" answers (13.7 %) also didn't say it isn't SNCF's official rate.

So even copying is too much to ask of Qwen here, probably because the limits come after the numbers it was looking for.

**Proposed fix: add the sentence in code, not through Qwen.** After the guard accepts an answer, `agent.py` adds the limits itself at the end, whenever `confidence_interval` was called in that question:

```
Le taux de retard … est de 9,5 %, entre 8,9 % et 10,3 %.      ← written by Qwen, checked by the guard

Limits: the data does not say why trains were late, nor whether a given
train will be late. A total over several routes is not SNCF's official
national rate (one train can count on several routes).          ← added by agent.py, always
```

- **Always there, always correct**, because Qwen plays no part in it. The same idea as the guard: enforce it in code, not in the prompt.
- **Simpler:** I'd remove the limits line from the tool output and the extra words in rule 7, so Qwen's job goes back to what it already does well.
- **Testable without Qwen:** a test in `test_agent.py` checks the limits are added after a `confidence_interval` call, and not to an answer like "hello".
- **One downside:** the limits are in English even when Qwen answers in French. That matches the app's UI, which is all English. A French version would mean guessing the question's language.

Should I switch to this?

```
"""The interactive full-screen app (Textual), like the Claude Code CLI.

Layout, from top to bottom:
- the history: banner, questions, tool calls, answers; it scrolls with the

- on the right, the panel (sidebar.py): session, conversations, starters.
```

Step 4 is done. The limits line is now built into the app, in the same style as Claude's "Claude can make mistakes". The tests pass (252). Nothing is committed yet.

**The text**, written in code (`DATA_LIMITS` in `ui.py`):
> *Monthly totals per route: they don't say why trains were late, or whether your train will be. A sum over routes is not SNCF's official national rate.*

The second sentence is your option 1 for the national rate.

**Where it appears:**

| Mode | Where |
|---|---|
| Full-screen app (`tgv`) | Always visible, dim and italic, at the bottom under the hint line |
| Line-by-line (`--simple`) and one question (`tgv "…"`) | Printed **once**, right after the startup banner |
| Inside answers | **Never**, so never under a greeting or a goodbye |

**Removed:** the extra line in the `confidence_interval` tool output, the "copy the limits" instruction in rule 7, and their 2 tests. Qwen's job is back to what it was, and it was getting that right.

**New tests:**
- the line is below the question box in the app, and it is not added to the conversation after an answer
- it's printed exactly once after the banner
- the text names both limits and contains no number

**For the defense:** the first attempt had Qwen copy the sentence from the tool, and it did so in **0 of 5** live answers. That's why it's now written in code. It's the same lesson as the guard: a rule written in code doesn't depend on Qwen. It also makes a good entry for your journal (« prompt réécrit … pourquoi »).

One more limit, "the interval assumes trains are independent, so the real uncertainty is wider", is too technical for the app's footer. I'll put it in `EVIDENCE.md` and the README.

```
"""Le même petit modèle, avec et sans harness, sur 6 questions dont on connaît la vraie réponse.

    python harness/comparer.py            # 3 essais par question et par niveau (quelques minutes)
    python harness/comparer.py --n 1      # un seul essai : plus rapide
```

## Claude · 2026-11-02 16:17

Here's the plan for brick 9. It follows the kit's `comparer.py`, the file the spec points to for this brick. Nothing is written yet.

## What the spec asks, and how we cover it

| Spec | Our version |
|---|---|
| « Au moins 10 tâches dont la réponse est connue » | **12 questions.** The true answer is computed **by the benchmark itself with pandas**, independently of the agent, as `comparer.py` does with `statistics` |
| « Votre système face à votre modèle seul » | **A = bare model:** Qwen alone, only the question, no tools, no data. **B = our system:** `run_agent` (tools + prompt + guard), thinking on as by default |
| « Au moins 3 lancements chacun » | 3 runs per question per system: 12 × 3 × 2 = **72 runs** |
| « Moyenne et dispersion » | Accuracy per run (out of 12) → **mean ± standard deviation over the 3 runs**, plus min–max; same for seconds and tokens |
| « Un échec expliqué à partir de sa trace » | Every run of B is saved in **`benchmark/traces/traces.jsonl`** (committed). I pick a failed run and explain it step by step from its trace |

**Bonus:** that committed trace file is also the evidence for **brick 1** (a real Qwen call to `run_python` with its result), so step 6 gets done along the way.

## Files
- **`benchmark/tasks.json`:** each task has its question, a short pandas expression that computes the true answer, and a tolerance (e.g. ±0.1 for a percentage).
- **`benchmark/run_benchmark.py`:** runs both systems, scores each answer, and writes the results.
- **`benchmark/results/results.csv`:** one row per run: task, system, run, correct?, status, seconds, turns, tokens, answer.
- **`benchmark/results/summary.md`:** the table of means and spreads, plus one row per question.
- **`tests/test_benchmark.py`:** tests for the scoring and the true answers on the fixture data, with no model (R2).

## Scoring (same rule as `comparer.py`)
An answer is **correct** if it contains a number equal to the true answer, within the tolerance. Numbers are read with our guard's `extract_numbers`, so "6 704" and "9,5" work. For B, an answer that was **blocked** or **unfinished** counts as wrong; otherwise the "Answer blocked: … 9.55" message could accidentally contain the right number.

**Known limit:** an answer that lists many numbers can be scored correct by luck. `comparer.py` has the same limit, and we'll say so.

## The 12 questions (a draft for you to adjust)
I picked questions whose answer is **a number** (no route names, no month names), so scoring is automatic:

| # | Question | What it tests |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | How many routes are in the dataset? | `describe_dataset` |
| 2 | Late rate Paris Lyon → Marseille St Charles, 2024 (FR) | our tuning question |
| 3 | Late rate Paris Montparnasse → Bordeaux St Jean, 2023 (EN) | another route, English |
| 4 | Late rate Lyon Part Dieu → Marseille St Charles, July 2025 | a single month |
| 5 | Share of all TGVs late at arrival in 2024 | the "national" sum (13.7 %) |
| 6 | Number of trains cancelled in 2023, all routes | a simple sum |
| 7 | Trains that ran Paris Est → Strasbourg in 2022 | sum with filters |
| 8 | Cancellation rate Paris Lyon → Nice Ville, 2024 | another rate (planned, not ran) |
| 9 | Trains more than 60 min late in 2024, all routes | another column |
| 10 | Late rate "Paris Gare de Lyon" → Marseille, 2023 | a station name not as written in the data |
| 11 | Late threshold in minutes for Paris Nord → Lille | the SNCF rule column (brick 6) |
| 12 | Which year had the highest overall late rate? | answer is a year, needs a group-by |

I'll check each true answer with pandas before running anything.

## Running time
- B takes about 50 s per run → 36 runs ≈ **30 min**.
- A takes about 5–10 s per run → about **5 min**.
- About **35–40 min in total**, run in the background, saving as it goes so an interruption loses nothing.

**Optional third system:** C = our system **with thinking off**, the faster mode. It would show whether thinking is worth the extra time, and adds about 15 min. It isn't required.

**Decisions for you:**
1. Keep, change or replace any of the 12 questions?
2. Add system C (thinking off), or only A and B?

## Claude · 2026-11-02 16:19

Reading the session 1 notebook (`cahier.html`, « Séance 1 · Le même modèle, avec et sans harness ») changes the plan. The kit doesn't compare 2 systems, it compares **3 levels of the same model**:

> « 0 · modèle seul — Rien. Il répond de mémoire. »
> « 1 · outil — Un outil, run_python : le modèle écrit du code, le harness l'exécute et lui renvoie ce que le code a affiché (ou l'erreur). »
> « 2 · contexte et garde-fous — On lui dit où sont les fichiers et à quoi ils ressemblent, on lui demande de calculer plutôt que de deviner, on limite le nombre de tours, on refuse une réponse donnée sans aucun calcul. »
> « Lancez le banc : python harness/comparer.py … Six questions dont la vraie réponse est calculée à part, le même modèle aux trois niveaux, un pourcentage de réponses justes par niveau. »

The point they make is « Le modèle n'a pas changé d'un niveau à l'autre », so the harness is what makes the difference. Our benchmark should show the same thing.

## The plan, rewritten to follow session 1

**3 levels, same model** (Qwen3.5-4B, thinking on):

| Level | What's around Qwen | How |
|---|---|---|
| **0 · bare model** | nothing: just the question, answered from memory | one `llm.chat` call, no tools |
| **1 · + tool** | only `run_python`, no system prompt, no guard | a small loop in `run_benchmark.py` (about 25 lines, like the kit's `repondre` at level 1) |
| **2 · full system** | our harness: system prompt + column list + 3 tools + guard + turn limit | `run_agent` as is |

`agent.py` doesn't change. The guard stays always on in the product, and level 1 has its own small loop inside the benchmark.

**Tasks:** 12 questions (draft below). **The true answer is computed separately** with pandas in the benchmark, as `comparer.py` does with `statistics`, never by the agent.

**Scoring:** the same rule as `comparer.juste`. An answer is correct if it contains a number equal to the truth within a tolerance, read with our guard's `extract_numbers` so "6 704" and "9,5" work. Two fairness rules:
- **Rates count in either form.** For rates, 9.5 and 0.0955 are both accepted, since levels 0 and 1 may answer as a fraction.
- **Level 2 blocked or unfinished answers count as wrong.** The "Answer blocked: … 9.55" message contains numbers that could match by luck.

**Runs:** 3 per question per level, 12 × 3 × 3 = **108 runs**. That's about **1 hour in the background** (level 0 ≈ 5 min, level 1 ≈ 20 min, level 2 ≈ 30 min), saving each run as it finishes so an interruption loses nothing.

**Output, laid out like `comparer.py`:**
```
question   truth    level 0   level 1   level 2
Q1          130       0/3       2/3       3/3
...
Total                 x/36      y/36      z/36
```
plus, per level: **mean accuracy ± standard deviation over the 3 runs**, min–max, mean seconds and tokens.

**Failure explained:** every level 1 and level 2 run goes into `benchmark/traces/traces.jsonl` (committed). The notebook names failures to look for: an invented answer at level 0, wrong column names at level 1, and at level 2 one that « "corrige" en supprimant un filtre et répond juste à côté ». I'll pick a real failure and explain it from its trace. That file is also the **brick 1** evidence.

**Files:**
- `benchmark/tasks.json`: questions, truth expressions, tolerances
- `benchmark/run_benchmark.py`: the 3 levels, scoring, summary
- `benchmark/results/results.csv`: one row per run
- `benchmark/results/summary.md`: the table, means and spreads
- `tests/test_benchmark.py`: scoring and truths on the fixture, no model (R2)

**The 12 questions (draft):**

| # | Question | What it tests |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | How many routes are in the dataset? | `describe_dataset` |
| 2 | Late rate Paris Lyon → Marseille St Charles, 2024 (FR) | our tuning question |
| 3 | Late rate Paris Montparnasse → Bordeaux St Jean, 2023 (EN) | another route, English |
| 4 | Late rate Lyon Part Dieu → Marseille St Charles, July 2025 | a single month |
| 5 | Share of all TGVs late at arrival in 2024 | the "national" sum |
| 6 | Trains cancelled in 2023, all routes | a simple sum |
| 7 | Trains that ran Paris Est → Strasbourg, 2022 | sum with filters |
| 8 | Cancellation rate Paris Lyon → Nice Ville, 2024 | another rate (planned, not ran) |
| 9 | Trains more than 60 min late in 2024, all routes | another column |
| 10 | Late rate "Paris Gare de Lyon" → Marseille, 2023 | station name not as in the data |
| 11 | Late threshold in minutes, Paris Nord → Lille | the SNCF rule (brick 6) |

## Brick 9 plan (final)

**The two systems, same model** (Qwen3.5-4B, thinking on):

| | What | How |
|---|---|---|
| **A · Qwen alone** | just the question, no tools, no data, no prompt; answers from memory | one `llm.chat` call |
| **B · Qwen + our harness** | system prompt + 3 tools + guard + turn limit | `run_agent` as is |

**The steps:**
1. **12 questions** (at least 10 required), each with its **true answer computed separately with pandas** in the benchmark, never by the agent.
2. **A:** 12 questions × 3 tries = 36 runs.
3. **B:** 12 questions × 3 tries = 36 runs.
4. **Scoring** (same rule as the kit's `comparer.py`): an answer is correct if it contains a number equal to the truth within a tolerance. For rates, 9.5 and 0.0955 both count. A blocked or unfinished B answer counts as wrong.
5. **Mean and spread:** for each system, accuracy for each of the 3 rounds (out of 12) → **mean ± standard deviation**, plus min–max. The same for seconds and tokens per answer. Plus a table per question (`x/3` for A and for B).
6. **Failure explained:** every B run is saved in `benchmark/traces/traces.jsonl` (committed). I pick at least one real B failure and explain it step by step from its trace. The spec asks for one; we can add more if they're instructive. That file is also the **brick 1** evidence.

- `benchmark/tasks.json`: questions, truth expressions, tolerances
- `benchmark/run_benchmark.py`: runs A and B, scores, writes the results
- `benchmark/results/results.csv`: one row per run
- `benchmark/results/summary.md`: mean ± spread table, per-question table
- `tests/test_benchmark.py`: scoring and truths tested on the fixture, no model (R2)

**Running time:** 72 runs. A takes about 5–10 s each and B about 50 s, so roughly **35–40 min**, in the background, saving each run as it goes.

```
df = pd.read_csv("data/clean/tgv_clean.csv")
def route(a, b): return (df.gare_depart == a) & (df.gare_arrivee == b)
def rate(m): d = df[m]; return d.nb_train_retard_arrivee.sum() / d.nb_circules.sum()

print("3", rate(route("PARIS MONTPARNASSE","BORDEAUX ST JEAN") & (df.year==2023)))

d = df[route("PARIS EST","STRASBOURG") & (df.year==2022)]; print("7", d.nb_circules.sum(), len(d))
d = df[route("PARIS LYON","NICE VILLE") & (df.year==2024)]; print("8", d.nb_annulation.sum()/d.nb_train_prevu.sum(), len(d))

d = df[route("PARIS NORD","LILLE")]; print("11", d.seuil_retard.unique(), d.duree_moyenne.min(), d.duree_moyenne.max(), len(d))

print("12", r.idxmax(), r.round(4).to_dict())
EOF
```
